2. RECOGNITION 
 
A. TSD Mission Moment – Schroeder Elementary Students 
 
Over the past several years, Schroeder Elementary has teamed up with 
IA-West and an organization called buildOn to help build schools in 
underprivileged countries. This year, Schroeder is helping to build a school 
in Haiti. Schroeder students have also helped build schools in Nicaragua 
and Nepal. To make this project possible, Schroeder organizes an annual 
Kids 4 K ids Sale to help raise money for building a new school. Two 
Schroeder parents, Mrs. Weallans and Mrs. Pingree , organize and chair 
the event. Schroeder students participate by making numerous donations 
such as toys, athletic equipment, DVD’s, games and many  other gently 
used items. Parents, students and community members are invited to 
shop at the sale, which is one of Schroeder’s most popular events! All of 
the money raised at the Kids 4 Kids Sale is donated to buildOn. Students 
from IA -West travel to the d esignated country to join the construction 
crew and actually participate in building the new school. Every year, IA 
students return to Schroeder and visit classrooms to show pictures of the 
new school. This wonderful event helps show how our elementary 
students can change the world and help those in need.  
 
 
RECOMMENDED ACTION:  Brian Canfield , two parents  and several 
Schroeder students will provide a short presentation to the Board.

B.  2014-2015 Outstanding Support Staff Award    
 
The Troy School District has many outstanding support staff employees. This evening 
we are proud to recog nize the accomplishments of thirteen  individuals who were 
nominated for the Troy School District Outstanding Support Staff Award. 
The nominees represent exemplary staff at every level and job description. They were 
nominated by their colleagues , by parents and by former and current students, all of 
whom talked about the impact these employees have on the lives of those around 
them. We are grateful for their dedication and passion for our students. 
Would you please come forward as your name is ca lled? (Distribute 
certificates/plants.)

2.       RECOGNITION 
 
 D.  National Teacher Appreciation Week 
 
Teacher Appreciation Week is observed nationally each year in May. This 
year, Teacher Appreciation Week is being celebrated May  4th – May 8th.  At 
tonight’s meeting , the Troy School District and Board of Education would 
like to recognize all of our outstanding teachers by adopting the following 
resolution: 
 
 
WHEREAS, throughout Troy and across the country, teachers inspire, 
nurture and open children’s minds to new ideas,  
 
WHEREAS, teachers promote and strengthen the principles of democratic 
equality, develop leaders and encourage critical thinking; and 
 
WHEREAS, Troy teachers set the bar for excellence in education and 
continually exceed the highest standards in the profession; and 
 
WHEREAS, the goal of Teacher Appreciation Week is to raise national and 
local awareness of teaching excellence in building a better society; now,  
 
THEREFORE BE IT  RESOLVED, that the Troy School District supports 
National Teacher Appreciation Week, May 4-8, 201 5, and encourages our 
schools and community to appropriately recognize the vital and changing 
role of teachers in imagining and creating a better future for society. 
 
 
RECOMMENDED ACTION:  Read and adopt resolution.

  Supported by:

TSD Mission Moment – Smith

Troy School District’s Outstanding 
Teachers 
 
 
 
 
 
Dr. Machesky introduced the Troy School District Mission 
Moment recognizing Calen Knight, a sixth grade student 
at Smith Middle School. She is being honored this 
evening for her efforts to create a charity to help make 
her community a better place to live. When Calen was 
seven, she decided to donate some of her stuffed animals 
and toys to children at Lighthouse of Oakland County. 
She wanted to help kids her age that didn’t have the 
things she did. Then she learned that children needed 
more so she started to gather clothing, winter coats, 
anything she discovered there was a need for. Eventually, 
Calen started her own charity, Calen4kids.org and began 
recruiting her classmates and friends to help.  Tonight, 
Calen was recognized as a leader in community outreach 
and charity for the entire region. Calen was asked to 
come up and talk a little more about her efforts to help 
children and families through Calen4kids.org. She shared 
a brief presentation with the Board and audience.  The 
Board commended her for her compassion and gave her 
a certificate of recognition and appreciation.

Enrollment Projections 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
It was moved by Mr. Hauff and supported by Ms.

Dr. Machesky presented a report on enrollment 
projections for the District.  He stated that he would be 
presenting an analysis of District enrollment history and 
projections, which was provided by Dr. Fred Ignatovich of 
Stanfred Consultants.  Dr. Machesky explained briefly 
about the data that is collected and analyzed for 
projecting enrollment numbers for public school districts. 
He then reviewed the historical data as well as 
information on schools of choice students in the District 
and the impact schools of choice has on the District 
related to our current level of enrollment.  He talked 
about the three scenarios of projection levels in the 
report – low, most likely and high and then explained 
17
Board of Education Meeting 6 April 14, 2015 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Reading Recovery TLL Pilot 
Update 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Board Comments 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
how the projections are calculated for enrollment using 
that specific formula. He reviewed the total projected 
enrollment for grades K – 12 for the 2015/2016 school 
year and provided graphs showing projections through 
the 2018/2019 school year. He mentioned the trend of 
declining enrollment through 2019/2020.    He completed 
his report by revisiting the enrollment numbers that had 
been projected for 2014/2015 at this time last year and 
then compared them to the actual enrollment numbers 
verified on official membership day.  He indicated that 
the District had 52 less students this year than the 
projected numbers and commented that Dr. Ignatovich is 
only able to go by live births from Oakland County and 
that perhaps families migrated out of the county during 
the recession.  Dr. Machesky shared that the District is 
now doing central enrollment and that it would give us a 
good chance to see how many new families are moving 
into the District.   
 
 
 
The District initiated a Pilot of Reading Recovery, Kinder-
Core, and Teaching and Learning Leaders at the 
beginning of the 2013-2014 school year.  The purpose of 
the pilot is to address the achievement gap by providing 
early intervention and expert instruction to struggling 
readers.  Mrs. Haezebrouck provided a brief update on 
how the pilot is progressing.  She talked about the 
Reading Recovery pilot timeline then reviewed reading 
recovery data pertaining to both this year and last year.   
Mrs. Haezebrouck shared information about the teaching 
and learning leaders and the role they have taken in the 
buildings.  She also shared thoughts in how the District is 
looking ahead following the pilot.  
 
Mr. Hauff mentioned that this was reviewed at great 
length at the March workshop and is sure this will help 
our students to succeed.  He asked how the District 
follows up with Reading Recovery students.  Mrs. 
Haezebrouck indicated that teachers touch base with one 
another and can include students in literacy groups if 
needed.   
 
Mr. Schmidt asked if there is any extra professional 
development offered for new kindergarten teachers to 
help get them up to speed with the other teachers.  Mrs. 
Haezebrouck indicated that all new teachers have 
18
Board of Education Meeting 7 April 14, 2015

mentors and that much instruction, modeling and 
coaching goes on between the mentors and teachers.   
They work harmoniously to support one another.  Mr. 
Schmidt asked if the literacy groups are research-based.  
Mrs. Haezebrouck confirmed that they are research-
based but that teachers can only take four students at a 
time and that the reading material is geared to their 
level.  Mr. Schmidt also inquired about whether the 
District has enough resources in the pilot to keep the 
ratio down.  Mrs. Haezebrouck shared that some schools 
have Title I support.  Mr. Schmidt inquired about the 
ratio.  Heidi Palaj indicated that they could provide data 
to the Board that can show progress made by students.   
 
Dr. Philippart mentioned that the Board had an 
opportunity to interact with the teachers during the 
March workshop and thanked Mrs. Haezebrouck and the 
teachers for their presentation. 
 
 
High school Biology teachers received biotechnology 
equipment as a part of the District’s Technology Bond 
and wanted to share the impact this cutting edge 
equipment has had on their students’ learning.  The 
Board welcomed District Science Coordinator, Jessica 
Ashley and Biology teachers Andrea Bolterstein and 
Jennifer Sprague who provided a little background on 
what biotechnology is and what their students are 
learning.   They shared a PowerPoint presentation with 
photos of the MiniOne System and diagrams of what 
students are working on.  They talked about the ways 
students use their new tools and how they are learning 
skills to help them to become critical thinkers.  The Board 
enjoyed looking at a couple samples the presenters 
shared to experience what the students use in class, 
including a taste test strip.   
 
Board members commented on how much they enjoyed 
the presentation and appreciated seeing how the bond 
was used to help student instruction.  Mrs. Ashley 
mentioned that the Troy School District is the first school 
district in the state of Michigan to have the MiniOne 
System.

visiting Forensics Science classes to share how evidence is

announced that Niles would be sponsoring a 5K Walk 
next month at Firefighters Park to help Wounded 
Warriors and they would also be hosting a blood drive on

th .

started M-step testing this week.  He mentioned that 
HOSA and DECA state competitions are coming up and 
that IA-East has 19 qualifiers this year.  He also shared 
that their Robotics team qualified for the international 
competition in St. Louis and that they are very proud of 
them.

they are having a successful season so far.  She added 
that seniors are making their final college decisions now.

and is going smoothly and that preparation for AP testing 
is happening.  She announced that Athens’ clubs are 
holding a fashion show, a blood drive and a dodge ball

community.

  MEETINGS OF THE BOARD     B-1200 
 
 
B-1210 CLOSED SESSIONS 
 
The Board may hold a closed session at any time, as long as public 
notice is provided as set forth in Section B-1201. 
 
The Board may meet in closed session only after the purpose of the 
closed session has been announced and (except for closed sessions 
held for the purposes set forth in subsections 1, 2, and 3 below) five 
affirmative votes by roll call have been obtained.  The purpose and 
the roll call vote must be entered into the minutes of the meeting. 
 
The Board may not make any decisions requiring a vote at a 
meeting which is closed to the public.  The closed meetings are only 
for deliberation, review, or negotiation. 
 
The purposes for which a closed session may be called are limited 
to the following: 
 
 1. To consider the dismissal, suspension, or disciplining of or to 
hear complaints or charges brought against or to consider a 
periodic personal evaluation of  a public officer, employee, staff 
member or individual agent, if the named person requests a 
closed hearing. 
 
 2. To consider the dismissal, suspension, or disciplining of a 
student and when the student or his parent or guardian 
requests a closed hearing. 
 
 3. For strategy and negotiation sessions connected with the 
negotiation of a collective bargaining agreement. 
 
 4. To consider the purchase or lease of real property up to the time 
an option to purchase or lease that real property is obtained. 
 
 5. To consult with the attorney regarding trial or settlement 
strategy in connection with specific pending litigation, but this 
should occur only when an open meeting would have a 
detrimental financial effect upon the litigating or settlement 
positions of the Board. 
  MEETINGS OF THE BOARD     B-1200 
 
123
 
 
B-1210 CLOSED SESSIONS (cont.)  
 
6. To review the specific contents of an application for 
employment or appointment to a public office, when the 
candidate requests that the application remain confidential.  
Interviews for employment or appointment to a public office 
will be held in an open meeting. 
 
7. To consider material exempt from discussion or disclosure by 
state or federal statute. 
 
Minutes of all closed sessions are recorded and include at least the 
following: 
 
1. The time, date, and place of the meeting. 
 
2. The members present and members absent. 
 
 
3.   Non-menbers present.  
 
34. The purpose or purposes for which the closed session is held.  
 
5.  Disciplinary hearing for a student or considering a complaint 
against an employee.   
 
 
These minutes are retained by the secretary of the Board or 
designee, are not available to the public, and are disclosed only if 
required by a civil action filed under Section 10, 11, or 13 of Act No. 
267, Public Acts 1976.  These minutes are destroyed one year and 
one day after approval of the minutes of the meeting at which the 
closed session was approved.

MINUTES     B-1400 
 
 
 
B-1401 MINUTES 
 
The minutes of Board meetings, except closed sessions, are 
recorded and printed for the secretary by the administrative staff 
and forwarded to members with the agenda for the next meeting 
for approval.  Corrected minutes shall show both the original entry 
and the correction. 
 
1. Minutes for all meetings include the following: 
 
 a. The time, date, and place of the meeting. 
 
 b. The members present and members absent. 
 
 c. A statement of the topics discussed and any decisions 
made at the meeting. 
 
 d. The purpose or purposes for which a closed session is held. 
 
 e.  All resolutions,  names of those who propose and second 
all resolutions, results of all votes taken including the 
number of those voting yes and no.  In the event of either a 
required roll call vote 
, or a non-unanimous vote, or an 
abstention, a roll call vote will be taken and the names of 
those voting yes and no will be recorded.  If a member 
abstains on a vote the reason for the abstention will be 
recorded.  The disposition of the vote will be noted.   
 
2. Proposed minutes must be available for public inspection at 
the address stated on the posted notice of the meeting within 
eight business days after a meeting.  Approved minutes must 
be available at the posted address within five business days 
after the meeting at which they are approved.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
127
 
 
MINUTES     B-1401 (cont.)  
  
 3.     All records of the Board are made available to the public in  
         compliance with the Michigan Freedom of Information Act,  
         Act No. 442, PA 1976.  
 
4.    The official minutes are bound and kept in the vault at the  
        administrative office.

RIGHTS AND RESPONSIBILITIES, CODE OF STUDENT CONDUCT    J-2000  
 
 
J-2012 ANTI-BULLYING POLICY  
It is the policy of the District to provide a safe educational environment for all 
students and employees.  Bullying of a student or employee at school, in any 
building within the Troy School District boundaries or any TSD event is strictly 
prohibited. This policy shall be interpreted and enforced to protect all students 
and employees to equally prohibit bullying without regard to its subject matter 
or motivating animus.   
A. 
 Prohibited Conduct.  
 
1.  Bullying.  Bullying of a student or employee is strictly prohibited.  For the 
purposes of this policy, "bullying" shall be defined as: 
 
Any written, verbal, or physical act, or any electronic communication, that is 
intended or that a reasonable person would know is likely to harm one or more 
students or employees either directly or indirectly by doing any of the 
following:   
 
a. Substantially interfering with educational opportunities, benefits, or 
programs of one or more students or employees;  
 
b. Adversely affecting a student or employee’s ability to participate in or 
benefit from the District's educational programs or activities by placing 
the student or employee in reasonable fear of physical harm or by 
causing substantial emotional distress;  
 
c. Having an actual and substantial detrimental effect on a student or 
employee's physical or mental health; or 
 
d. Causing substantial disruption in, or substantial interference with, the  
 orderly operation of the school.   
 
2.  Retaliation/False Accusation.   Retaliation or false accusation against a target of 
bullying, anyone reporting bullying, a witness, or another person with reliable 
information about an act of bullying is strictly prohibited.   
 
B. Reporting an Incident .  If a student, staff member, or other individual believes 
there has been an incident of bullying in violation of this policy, s/he shall promptly 
report such incident to the appropriate principal or designee,  
129
 C. Investigation.   All reported allegations of a policy violation or related 
complaint about bullying shall be promptly and thoroughly investigated by the 
building principal or designee.  Each verified incident of bullying shall be 
documented and the resulting consequences including discipline and referrals will be 
reported annually to the school board. 
D. Notice to Parent/Guardian.  If the principal or designee determines that an 
incident of bullying has occurred, s/he shall promptly provide notification of same to 
the parent/guardian of the victim of the bullying and the parent/guardian of the 
perpetrator of the bullying.   
E. Annual Reports.  At least annually, the building principal or designee, or the 
Responsible School Official shall report all verified incidents of bullying and the 
resulting consequences, including any disciplinary action or referrals, to the Board of 
Education.   
F. Responsible School Official.   The superintendent shall be responsible for 
ensuring the proper implementation of this policy throughout the District.  The 
foregoing appointment shall not reduce or eliminate the duties and responsibilities of 
a principal or designee as described in this policy. 
G.  Posting/Publication of Policy.  Notice of this policy will be: (a) annually 
circulated to all students and staff, (b) posted in conspicuous locations in all school 
buildings and departments within the District, (c) annually discussed with students 
and employees, and (d) incorporated into the teacher, student, and parent/guardian 
handbooks. 
H.  Definitions  
1. "At school" means in a classroom, elsewhere on school premises, on a school 
bus or other school-related vehicle, or at a school-sponsored activity or event 
whether or not it is held on school premises.  "At school" also includes any 
conduct using a telecommunications access device  or telecommunications service 
provider  that occurs off school premises if the device or provider is owned by or 
under the District's control. 
2. "Telecommunications Access Device" means that term as defined in Section 
219a of the Michigan Penal Code, 1931 PA 328, MCL 750.219a, as may be 
amended from time to time.   
 
3. "Telecommunications Service Provider" means that term as defined in Section 
219a of the Michigan Penal Code, supra , as may be amended from time to time.   
 
A

6. A.  PERSONNEL 
 
 CONSIDERATION:  TEA CONTRACT RATIFICATION 
 
A tentative agreement between representatives of the Troy Board of Education 
and the Troy Education Association/MEA/NEA (TEA) was reached on April 22, 
2015.  The TEA membership voted to ratify the agreement on April 30, 
2015.  The tentative agreement extends the contract through June 30, 2016.  
 
The agreement provides for step improvements on the exist ing 14 -step salary 
schedule, a  Board-paid annuity as defi ned; adjustments for health insurance 
payments as  permitted under the provisions of Public Act 152 and the 
continuation of two additional instruction days on the school calendar.  
 
Other than language deletions required by law, all other terms and conditions 
remain unchanged from the prior agreement.  
 
 
 
 RECOMMENDED ACTION:  Accept the resolution below.

 Supported by: 
 
 
 
 
 Be it therefore resolved that the Board of Education hereby ratifies and adopts 
the tentative agreement reached on April 22, 2015 between representatives of the 
Troy Board of Education and the Troy Education Association/MEA/NEA for the 
period of April 30, 2014 through June 30, 2016.

 A. REPORT:   Special Ed Review  
 
An external review , led  by Ms. Donna Tinberg of Thayer -North Educational 
Consulting LLC, was recently conducted on special education services in the Troy 
School District.  The purpose of the study was to provide an outside perspective 
on the delivery  of special education services  within our District .  The review 
included a study of district data and documents along with focus group meetings 
and site visits to buildings representing all gra de levels from preschool through 
post-secondary.  Mrs. Kathy Jagels is here with us this evening to provide an 
update on the review.   
 
 
 
  RECOMMENDED ACTION:  This a report – no action is necessary.

7. CURRICULUM 
 
 B. REPORT:   ELD Program Review  
 
Since the Troy School District’s fastest growing subgroup is our English Learner 
population, an external consultation was arranged with a national expert the field 
of English Language Lea rners to discuss the vision of the English Language 
Program in our District  and develop a plan to bridge our current program with 
our recommended future .  Meetings were held to review the District’s 
foundation, framework and tools being used in our current program.  Mrs. Mary 
Haezebrouck and Mrs. Tammy DiPonio are here with us this evening to share the 
results of this consultation.   
 
 
 
  RECOMMENDED ACTION:  This a report – no action is necessary.

#5 Appoint ELD Administrator   
• OCR recommends a 
coordinator/director/administrator to oversee the 
program and ensure we are in compliance with 
the delivery of services. 
• This person must be knowledgeable of all 
programming and Title III grants. 
• Partial salary may be funded through the Title III 
grant.

169
Recommendation from the Review 
 #6 Hire Additional ELD Specialists  
to Comply with Federal, State, and County Mandates  
• Currently, the teacher/student ELD ratio in Troy is 
1/161 - 1/204. 
• Currently, elementary ELD teachers travel 
between two buildings. 
• When ELD teachers are at their other assigned 
building, students are serviced by para-educators. 
• According to the DOJ, para-educators need to be 
under the direct supervision of the ELD teachers.

(i.e.:  retired teachers, para-educators) 
• When? 
–Beginning of  the year for new students 
–Yearlong – for enrollment testing  
–Spring testing – WIDA Access

7. C.   05.05.15 
 
 
7. CURRICULUM 
 
 C. REPORT:   ELD Instructional Materials Recommendation 
 
The State of Michigan announced that they were joining the World -Class 
Instructional Design and Assessment  (WIDA) consortium in January of 2013. 
WIDA Standards have  an explicit connection to Common Core State Standards  
(CCSS) and are the s tepping stones to help students access the CCSS.  Due to 
these new State standards, greater diversity within the District, and our growing 
English Learner population, it has been re commended the District procure new 
instructional materials .  ELD Specialists, Ms. Christin Silagy and Ms. Danielle 
Andresen are here with us this evening to introduce this new curriculum.    
 
 
 
  RECOMMENDED ACTION:  This a report – no action is necessary. 
  
179
Elementary English Language 
Development Program 
Recommendations 
May 5, 2015 
 
180
English Language Development 
Standards in Michigan 
2 
 The State of Michigan announced that they were joining the 
World-Class Instructional Design and Assessment (WIDA) 
consortium in January of 2013. There was no ‘transition’ to 
these new standards. Assessment of these standards began in 
September of 2013.  
 
 Prior to WIDA Standards, Michigan had their own English 
Language Proficiency Standards.  
181
Differences 
3 
English Language Proficiency  
Standards 
 All standards were based in 
reading, writing, speaking and 
listening 
 All standards were based in the 
content area of English/Language 
Arts 
 All standards were assessed 
through English Language Arts 
World-Class Instructional Design 
and Assessment Standards 
 All standards are based in reading, 
writing, speaking and listening 
 All content areas are included in the 
standards 
 Social Instructional Language 
 The Language of Language Arts 
 The Language of Mathematics 
 The Language of Science 
 The Language of Social Studies 
 All standards are assessed through 
content area 
182
Alignment with the  
Michigan State Standards 
4 
WIDA Standards have an explicit connection to Common 
Core State Standards 
 
WIDA Standards are stepping stones to help students 
access the Michigan State Standards (MSS) 
183
Need For a New Curriculum 
5 
 New standards –old program not aligned to WIDA or MSS 
 Achievement Gap 
 Growing and increasingly diverse English Learner (EL) population  
 80+ languages in district 
 30+ languages in some buildings 
 Interrupted schooling 
 Refugees 
 Increase in Non-English speakers 
184
Recommended Curriculum 
6 
 National Geographic REACH Levels A-C 
 Grammar Gallery 
185
National Geographic Reach 
7 
 
 National Geographic REACH Language-Literacy-Content’s 
program is aligned to the K-5 MSS, WIDA and State GLCE 
 
 The intent is to actively engage and immerse EL students in a 
content-based ELD approach merging much needed language 
instruction with the content areas, while meeting standards. This is 
accomplished through included-differentiation strategies so that all 
students’ needs are met.  
 
186
National Geographic Reach 
8 
 National Geographic REACH Language-Literacy-Content easily 
adapts to meet our instructional priorities and requirements. It is a 
research-based program that was designed by six of the leading 
educators in the field of English language development, including 
Dr. Deborah Short, co-developer of the SIOP Model. 
 
 Each unit within the program is built around a Big Question to 
incorporate language, vocabulary, reading and writing. Big 
Questions help spur ongoing student discussion and dialogue and 
develop high levels of academic talk. Not only do they align to 
State adopted standards, but they also encourage ELs to think 
critically about new ideas and concepts, express their ideas and 
interests, help set authentic purposes for reading, and inquire about 
their world. 
 
187
National Geographic Reach 
 Features robust science and social studies units that address the 
language needs of ELs with a strong concentration on vocabulary 
development.  The student and teacher components of the program 
all work together to fulfill this purpose. 
 
 Reach includes placement, formative, benchmark and summative 
assessments to make sure students are on the correct path to 
English language development.  
 
9 
188
 The program fosters exposure to a wide range of topics and 
provides students with multiple opportunities to develop 
background knowledge in the process. These opportunities 
include: technological resources for building background; 
kinesthetic and cooperative activities that tap prior knowledge 
and experience; concept-focused visual and contextualized 
vocabulary resources; frequent opportunities to listen and 
engage in oral language activities; and frequent use of graphic 
organizers to organize, build, and share knowledge. 
 
10 
National Geographic Reach 
189
Grammar Gallery 
11 
 Grammar Gallery is aligned to the K-12 MSS and WIDA. 
 
 The co-authors Dr. Roberta Stathis and Patrice Gotsch have 
extensive knowledge of the needs of English Learners. They have 
been involved in best practice research around ELs for more than 
15 years.  
 
 The intent is to provide students with explicit grammar instruction. 
Grammar instruction is integrated in all four language domains 
(reading, writing, speaking, and listening) as well as the Language, 
Reading and Writing strands of the MSS. 
 
 Grammar Gallery is a core or supplemental program that can be 
used to build vocabulary, teach grammar and devolve academic 
language proficiency. “No one is a native speaker of academic 
language.” 
 
190
Grammar Gallery 
 Features a grammar library that includes students’ reference sheets 
and activities that cover each grammatical form. It also features a 
reading gallery that includes readings (fiction and nonfiction) and 
activities incorporated into the reinforced lessons. They also have a 
writing gallery that includes writing resources and practice 
activities incorporated into the EXPAND lessons.  
 
 Grammar Gallery includes 20,000 full-color photographs and 
illustrations. It has loads of support materials including pre and 
post checks, extension activities, picture prompts, flexible frames 
and games.  
 
12 
191
Grammar Gallery 
13 
 
 Grammar Gallery units are arranged by proficiency level. Below 
each proficiency level are six topics or themes. Each topic or 
theme contains introduction lessons, reinforcement lessons and 
expanded lessons. These lessons contain an overview, sentence 
frames, reading, critical thinking, academic vocabulary, 
comprehensions checks, and writing.  
 
 Students can access all aspects of the program on their iPads, on a 
computer, or through printouts.  
 
 This program would available to ALL teachers K-12. 
192
Process Before Recommendation 
14 
 
 Reviewed program samples and contacted two publishers 
to start a formal pilot (National Geographic and Houghton 
Mifflin Harcourt). 
 
 All elementary ESL teachers taught units from both 
programs. 
 
 Meetings were held to review, compare and evaluate 
programs. 
 
193
Process Before Recommendation 
 Reach was selected and it was noted that all programs had 
a grammar hole. 
 
 Contacted all publishers of grammar programs that were 
aligned with WIDA and MSS. 
 
 Meetings were held to review, compare, and evaluate 
grammar programs. 
 
 Grammar Gallery was selected.  
 
15 
194
Thank you!   
What questions do you have? 
16 
195

7. CURRICULUM  
 
D.  CONSIDERATION:  Elementary Block Schedule Pilot Program 
 
Beginning with the 2013-2014 school year, the District established an elementary 
block  schedule pilot program affecting specials instruction at grades K - 5, which 
has provided  enhanced instruction to students and is reviewed and improved.  It 
would be appropriate to officially formalize the Distr ict’s designation of the pilot 
program by Board action. 
 
RECOMMENDED ACTION: Adopt the following resolution. 
 
SUGGESTED RESOLUTION 
Moved by ________________________   
Supported by ______________________  
 
Be it therefore resolved that effective with the 201 3-2014 school year, an 
elementary  block schedule pilot program was established, relating to K - 5 
specials instruction, in order to enhance the delivery of specials and core 
curriculum, to be reviewed annually, with periodic reports provided to the Board. 
This Resolution is adopted in acco rdance with the Michigan Revised School Code  
and  section 15(3)(h) of the Michigan Public Employment Relations Act, and 
supersedes any other or conflicting resolutions, which are hereby rescinded. 
  Yes: _____________ 
 No: _____________

4400 Livernois

Mr. West: 
 
Over the past several months, we have been evaluating technology purchases in 
support of several initiatives.  Included in this are the following: 
 
 Video Surveillance equipment to continue to secure Troy Schools.  This is being 
done in collaboration with the Troy Police Department 
 Procurement of 3800 iPads and associated covers for finalizing the 1:1 initiative 
for grades 3-8 
 Airwatch MDM Software purchase to support the deployment of the additional 
iPads 
 Replacement of Uninterruptible Power Supplies to support phone operations in all 
buildings 
 
Each of these purchases is being brought to the Board for approval.  The purchasing 
vehicles on each are different but have been thoroughly reviewed by Kris Young and 
me to ensure that in each instance, the items are being purchased from a competitive 
contract.  The individual resolutions spell out the purchasing vehicle for the respective 
items.  In most cases, the items are being purchased from a contract and/or Vendor 
that we have purchased these items from previously. 
 
We are recommending that the District accept the proposals from the various suppliers 
and award the contracts in the amount of $1,940,978.41 as outlined below. 
 
Item Vendor Amount 
Video Surveillance SimplexGrinnell $325,507.00 
iPads Apple $1,437,196.00 
iPad Covers CDWG $87,400.00 
Airwatch Licenses CDWG $53,088.00 
UPS Systems SEHI, Inc. $37,787.41

     8. J. 05.05.15 
 
8. BUSINESS 
  
J.  REPORT:  Proposed Oakland Intermediate School District 
Budget 
 
Effective in 2004, Section 624 of the Revised School Code made it a 
requirement for every Intermediate School District to submit a copy 
of its budget to local school districts no later than May 1.  Local 
districts are required to pass a resolution either i n support or 
disapproval of the budget no later than June 1.  Rick West  will 
present the budget to the Board. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 RECOMMENDED ACTION: 
 
This report will be given by Rick West.  No action is required.


Oakland Schools Proposed Budgets
Overview of Fiscal Year 2015‐2016
274
ISD Budget Resolution
Revised School Code 380.624 ‐ Overview
1. Not later than May 1 of each year, the ISD shall submit proposed 
budget for next fiscal year to the board of each constituent district for 
review
2. Not later than June 1 of each year, the board of each constituent 
district shall review the proposed ISD budget, shall adopt a board 
resolution expressing its support for or disapproval of the proposed 
budget
3. Mail copy of resolution indicating support or disapproval (with 
rationale) to the Oakland Schools Board of Education

8. BUSINESS 
 
 K. CONSIDERATION:  Approve Oakland Intermediate School 
District 2015-2016 Budget 
 
  In accordance with Section 624 of the Revised School Code, as amended, 
the Troy Board of Education is required to review the proposed Oakland 
Intermediate School District budget and express either its support or 
disapproval of this budget.   
 
 
RECOMMENDED ACTION:  Adopt the following resolution. 
 
 
   Moved by  
 
 
   Supported by  
 
 
WHEREAS: 
 
 1. Section 624 of the Revised School Code, as amended, requires the 
intermediate school board to submit its proposed budget no later than May 1 
of each year to the board of each constituent district for review; and 
 
 2. Not later than June 1 of each year, the board of each constituent district shall 
review the proposed intermediate school district budget, shall adopt a board 
resolution expressing its support for or d isapproval of the proposed 
intermediate school district budget, and shall submit to the intermediate 
school board any specific objections and proposed changes the constituent 
district board has to the budget. 
 
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED THAT: 
 
 1. The Troy Board of Education has received and reviewed the proposed 
intermediate school district budget in accordance with Section 624 of the 
Revised School Code, as amended, and, by the adoption of this resolution, 
expresses its support for the proposed interm ediate school district budget.  
The Troy School District Board of Education believes that it is the responsibility 
288
of the Intermediate School District to be responsible for its own operations, 
including adoption of its budget.  The Troy School District’s s upport of the 
Oakland Intermediate School District’s budget is for the sole purpose of 
complying with Section 624 of the Revised School Code. 
 
 2. The secretary of the Troy Board of Education or his/her designee shall forward 
a copy of this resolution to the intermediate school board or its superintendent 
no later than June 1, 2015. 
 
 3. All resolutions insofar as they conflict with this resolution be and the same are 
hereby rescinded. 
 
 
Yes:  
 
No:  
 
 
Resolution declared adopted. 
 
 
  
 Secretary, Troy Board of Education 
 
The undersigned duly qualified and acting Secretary of the Board of Education of the 
Troy School District, Michigan, hereby certifies that the foregoing is a true and 
complete copy of a resolution adopted by the Troy Board of Education at a regular 
meeting held on May 5, 2015, the original of which resolution is part of the Board’s 
minutes, and further certifies that notice of the meeting was given to the public 
under the Open Meetings Act, 1976 PA 267, as amended.

10. OTHER 
 
 B.  FIRST READING:   Revisions to Board Policy B-2002 Compensation 
        and Expenses 
  
 Members of the Board of Education have reviewed and proposed changes to 
update Board Policy B-2002, Compensation and Expenses.  The first reading of 
this policy is presented at this evening’s meeting.   
  
 
RECOMMENDED ACTION:  A Board member  will read changes to Board policy B-2002. 
299
MEMBER DEVELOPMENT OPPORTUNITIES    B-2000  
 
 
 
B-2002 COMPENSATION AND EXPENSES 
 
Rate of compensation for Board members shall be $100 per annum.  
Members on school district business will be reimbursed for actual 
and necessary expenses incurred in the discharge of official duties 
provided one or both of the following conditions are met: (1) the 
Board, by a majority vote of its members at an open meeting, 
approved reimbursement of the specific expense before the expense 
was incurred, or (2) the expense is consistent with 
a policy adopted 
by the Board establishing specific categories of reimbursable 
expenses, and the Board approves the reimbursement before it is 
actually paid administrative guidelines for reimbursable expenses .  
Each member is responsible for submitting an itemized statement 
of reimbursable expenses to the assistant superintendent—business 
services for listing with the regular monthly bills.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Adopted: January 16, 1979 
Revised: March 17, 1998 
 April 15, 2003 
300
